Statistically and Computationally Optimal Estimation and Inference of Common Subspaces
Joshua Agterberg

Abstract
Given multiple data matrices, many problems in statistics and data science rely on estimating a common subspace that captures certain structure shared by all the data matrices. In this paper we investigate the statistical and computational limits for the common subspace model in which one observes a collection of symmetric low-rank matrices perturbed by noise, where each low-rank matrix shares the same common subspace. Our main results identify several regimes of the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) such that estimation and inference are statistically or computationally optimal, and we refer to these regimes as weak SNR, moderate SNR, strong estimation SNR, and strong inference SNR. First, we propose an estimator based on projected gradient descent initialized via spectral sum of squares and show that it achieves the optimal sin ⁡ Θ \sin\Theta error rate under strong estimation SNR. These results are complemented by both statistical and computational lower bounds identifying the weak and moderate estimation SNR regimes. Next, we turn to statistical inference for the sin ⁡ Θ \sin\Theta distance itself, and we show that our estimator has an asymptotically Gaussian distribution in the strong inference SNR regime. Based on this limiting result we propose confidence intervals and show that they are adaptively minimax optimal in the strong inference SNR regime, where adaptivity is measured in terms of the SNR. Finally, we show that adaptive confidence intervals are information-theoretically impossible below the strong inference SNR regime. Consequently, our results unveil a novel phenomenon: despite the SNR being “above” the computational limit for estimation, adaptive statistical inference may still be information-theoretically impossible.



Introduction

In many statistical problems of interest, one is provided with multiple datasets that are posited to share some common underlying structure, and the goal is to perform estimation and inference on this shared structure. Examples include neuroscience
(
63
)
, single-cell RNA sequencing
(
56
)
, and multilayer network analysis
(
59
;
57
;
49
;
54
)
. In many such settings, this shared structure manifests through linear-algebraic relationships between datasets, often in the form of subspaces or matrix factorizations. For instance, in statistical network analysis, it is natural to posit shared latent structure, such as common community memberships associated with the same set of vertices, across multiple observed networks
(
59
;
42
)
. Motivated by these considerations, in this paper we study how to optimally extract such common structure from multiple symmetric matrices.

To be more precise, in this paper we consider the
common subspace model
, first introduced in
8
for network data as the
Common Subspace Independent Edge
(COSIE) model. Suppose one observes
L
matrices
\{\bm{A}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{L}
with each
\bm{A}^{(l)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
such that

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=\bm{S}^{(l)}+\bm{N}^{(l)},

(1)

where
\bm{S}^{(l)}
(the signal matrix) is a symmetric rank
r
matrix, and
\bm{N}^{(l)}
is a symmetric noise matrix. We assume that
\bm{N}^{(l)}
has independent subgaussian entries, with variance
\sigma^{2}
on the diagonal and
\sigma^{2}/2
on the off-diagonal; that is, each
\bm{N}^{(l)}
is a Wigner matrix. In the case that each entry is Gaussian, we say it is a
{\sf GOE}
matrix (i.e., a member of the
Gaussian orthogonal ensemble
) with variance
\sigma^{2}
, otherwise we say it is a Wigner matrix with variance
\sigma^{2}
.

We further assume that each
\bm{S}^{(l)}
can be factorized via

\displaystyle\bm{S}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=\bm{U}\bm{R}^{(l)}\bm{U}^{\top},

where
\bm{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
is an orthonormal matrix common to all signal matrices (the
common subspace
), and
\bm{R}^{(l)}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
is a symmetric full-rank matrix associated to each
\bm{S}^{(l)}
. The matrices
\bm{R}^{(l)}
need not be diagonal. In general,
\bm{U}
and
{\bm{R}^{(l)}}
are identifiable only up to a simultaneous
r\times r
orthogonal transformation. For simplicity, we assume throughout that the rank
r
is known. The matrix
\bm{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
can be viewed as a matrix whose rows are
r
-dimensional latent Euclidean vectors associated to all the matrices
simultaneously
, and thus is an object of central importance when seeking to aggregate information from multiple datasets.

The common subspace model encompasses several widely studied network models as special cases, including the multilayer stochastic blockmodel, the multilayer mixed-membership blockmodel, and the multilayer degree-corrected mixed-membership blockmodel with common degree corrections. We refer the reader to
8
for more details on this model and its relation to other network models. However, the common subspace model need not be restricted to network data. Indeed, our formulation differs slightly from that work in that we assume independent subgaussian Wigner noise with common variance
\sigma^{2}
, which implicitly allows the signal matrices
\bm{S}^{(l)}
to be arbitrary symmetric low-rank matrices. By contrast, when
\bm{N}^{(l)}
consists of mean-zero Bernoulli noise, as is standard in network data applications, the entries of
\bm{S}^{(l)}
must lie in
[0,1]
, and hence the signal matrices are necessarily constrained.

The subgaussian noise assumption serves as a stylized statistical model for studying common subspace estimation in many settings. In the special case
L=1
, the model reduces to the classical matrix denoising problem. It is well known that the information-theoretical limits of estimation in such problems are governed by the signal strength of the underlying low-rank matrix, typically characterized by the magnitude of its smallest nonzero eigenvalue. In this paper, we investigate the information-theoretical limits of estimation and inference in the common subspace model in the regime where
L\gg 1
.

Main Contributions

Our main contributions in this paper are multifold. From a pragmatic perspective, one primary contribution of this paper is to develop and analyze an end-to-end procedure, projected gradient descent with spectral initialization, that aims to recover the shared subspace
\bm{U}
. While variants of our algorithm have been analyzed previously in the literature
(
59
)
, a more comprehensive statistical analysis under the common subspace model is still lacking. Furthermore, existing results often require additional assumptions on either the initialization or the structure of the signal matrices
\bm{S}^{(l)}
. In this work we only impose minimal signal-strength assumptions, and our results are complemented by the requisite lower bounds.

In this work we study the error
\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t},\bm{U})\|_{F}
, where
\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}
is the output of projected gradient descent after
t
iterations; this error is defined in
Section

1.3
. This loss function computes errors modulo orthogonal transformation, thus eliminating the intrinsic nonidentifiability inherent in the model. First, we show that
\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t},\bm{U})\|_{F}
converges to the optimal statistical error after logarithmically many iterations under a certain signal-to-noise (SNR) condition. Next, turning to statistical inference, we provide a novel limit theorem and propose confidence intervals for the error
\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t},\bm{U})\|_{F}^{2}
under a slightly stronger SNR condition. Remarkably, we demonstrate that we are able to provide confidence intervals for the loss without knowledge of
\bm{U}
, akin to the out-of-sample error in linear regression.

The other main contribution of this paper is to provide a comprehensive picture of the statistical and computational limits of estimation and inference. To the best of our knowledge, a minimax study for statistical
inference
of subspaces has not been studied previously. Our analysis reveals a novel phenomenon, which we can summarize in the following theorem. For simplicity of presentation we assume that
L\lesssim n
, that
r=O(1)
, and that each
\bm{N}^{(l)}
is Gaussian noise. In order to state our result, we define the following signal-strength parameter
\lambda
defined through the equation

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\lambda_{\min}\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{L}(\bm{R}^{(l)})^{2}\bigg).

Evidently, when
L=1
, the parameter
\lambda
is the magnitude of the smallest nonzero eigenvalue of the low-rank matrix.
We also define the
generalized condition number
via

\kappa:=\max_{l}\frac{\|\bm{R}^{(l)}\|}{\lambda}.

We are now prepared to state our main informal result.

Theorem 1

(Informal Statement of Main Results)
.

Suppose we observe
\{\bm{A}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{L}
as in (
1
), and assume that
\kappa
and
r
are bounded, and each
\bm{N}^{(l)}
is a
{\sf GOE}
matrix with variance
\sigma^{2}
. Then we have the following:

•

(Weak Estimation SNR) When
\lambda/\sigma\ll\sqrt{\frac{n}{L}}
, estimation of
\bm{U}
is information-theoretically impossible.

•

(Moderate Estimation SNR) When
\frac{\sqrt{n}}{L^{1/4}}\gg\lambda/\sigma\gtrsim\sqrt{\frac{n}{L}}
, no polynomial-time estimator of
\bm{U}
exists based on predictions from the low-degree likelihood ratio.

•

(Strong Estimation SNR) When
\lambda/\sigma\gtrsim\frac{\sqrt{n}}{L^{1/4}}
, projected gradient descent with spectral initialization achieves the optimal error rate after logarithmically many iterations.

•

(Weak Inference SNR) When
\frac{n}{\sqrt{L}}\gg\lambda/\sigma\gg\sqrt{\frac{n}{L}}
, minimax-optimal adaptive inference is information-theoretically impossible.

•

(Strong Inference SNR) When
\lambda/\sigma\gg\frac{n}{\sqrt{L}}
, adaptive statistical inference is information-theoretically possible, and our proposed confidence intervals adaptively achieve the optimal rate.

One immediate takeaway from this result is that the computational barrier to estimation occurs at a smaller SNR value than the information-theoretical barrier to adaptive statistical inference. Consequently, even if optimal
estimation
is feasible in polynomial time, adaptive
inference
may not be possible. However, whenever estimation or adaptive inference is both statistically and computationally feasible, our estimators achieve the optimal rates. These phase transition phenomena can be displayed concisely in the following diagram.

SNR
=\lambda/\sigma
0
\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{L}}
\frac{\sqrt{n}}{L^{1/4}}
\frac{n}{\sqrt{L}}
Weak Estimation SNR
Moderate Estimation SNR
Strong Estimation SNR
Weak Inference SNR
Strong Inference SNR

To the best of our knowledge, this is the first time that a problem of this form has exhibited both a “statistical-computational” estimation gap as well as a “computational-statistical” inferential gap (i.e., where the computational threshold for estimation lies below the statistical threshold for adaptive inference). In contrast, many existing models often have the property that being “above” the computational threshold for estimation results in statistical inference “for free,” in the sense that optimal inference comes with no additional SNR requirements, such as in tensor data analysis
(
65
)
.

Paper Organization

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In the subsequent section we introduce our main methodology. In
Section

3
we study the estimation error of our procedure, including an upper bound for each iteration and minimax and computational lower bounds. In
Section

4
we study the information-theoretical limits for (adaptive) statistical inference, and we provide minimax-optimal confidence intervals as well as information-theoretical lower bounds. In
Section

5
we discuss related work, and in
Section

6
we consider applications to real and simulated data. We finish with a discussion. Proofs of main results can be found in the appendices.

Notation

For two sequences
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
, we write
a_{n}\ll b_{n}
if
a_{n}/b_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, and we write
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
if there is a constant
C>0
such that
a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
. We will also write
a_{n}=o(b_{n})
if
a_{n}\ll b_{n}
and
a_{n}=O(b_{n})
if
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
. We write
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
if both
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
and
b_{n}\lesssim a_{n}
. For a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}\to\mathbb{R}
, we let
\nabla f
denote its gradient viewed as an
n\times r
matrix, and we let
\nabla^{2}f
denote its Hessian, viewed as either an operator on
n\times r
matrices or a
nr\times nr
matrix, where the distinction is clear from context.

For a general matrix
\bm{M}
we let
\|\bm{M}\|
and
\|\bm{M}\|_{F}
denote its spectral and Frobenius norms respectively, and we let
{\sf Tr}(\bm{M})
denote its trace. We let
{\sf SVD}_{r}(\bm{M})
denote the leading
r
left singular vectors of the matrix
\bm{M}
. For two orthonormal matrices
\bm{U}_{1}
and
\bm{U}_{2}
of dimension
n\times r
with
r\leq n
, we let
\sin\Theta(\bm{U}_{1},\bm{U}_{2})
denote the matrix of singular values of
(\bm{U}_{1})_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{U}_{2}
, where
(\bm{U}_{1})_{\perp}
denotes any matrix with orthonormal columns such that
(\bm{U}_{1})_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{U}_{1}=0
. In particular, we have that

\displaystyle\|\sin\Theta(\bm{U}_{1},\bm{U}_{2})\|_{F}=\|(\bm{U}_{1})_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{U}_{2}\|_{F}.

(2)

We let
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{U}_{1},\bm{U}_{2}}
denote the Frobenius-optimal orthogonal matrix aligning
\bm{U}_{1}
and
\bm{U}_{2}
; that is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\bm{U}_{1},\bm{U}_{2}}:=\argmin_{\mathcal{O}:\mathcal{OO}^{\top}=\bm{I}_{r}}\|\bm{U}_{1}\mathcal{O}-\bm{U}_{2}\|_{F}.

For orthonormal
\bm{U}_{1}
and
\bm{U}_{2}
, it is well-known that
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{U}_{1},\bm{U}_{2}}
can be computed from the product of the left and right singular vectors of
\bm{U}_{1}^{\top}\bm{U}_{2}
.

For a random variable
X
, we let
\|X\|_{\psi_{\alpha}}
denote its Orlicz
\psi_{\alpha}
norm. See
64
for more details. For a sequence of random variables
\{X_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
, we say
X_{n}\xrightarrow{d}X
if
X_{n}
converges to
X
in distribution. Similarly, we write
X_{n}\xrightarrow{p}X
if
X_{n}
converges in probability to
X
. For two random variables
X
and
Y
, we write
\mathbb{E}X
as the expected value of
X
and
\mathbb{E}X|Y
as the conditional expectation of
X
given
Y
.

Projected Gradient Descent with Spectral Initialization

In this section we introduce our main algorithm to estimate the matrix
\bm{U}
. Define the loss function

\displaystyle h\big(\bm{\widehat{U}};\{\bm{\widehat{R}}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{L}\big):=\frac{1}{4L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\|\bm{A}^{(l)}-\bm{\widehat{U}}\bm{\widehat{R}}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\|_{F}^{2}.

Up to possible rescaling,
h
can be viewed as the objective function whose minimizer is the maximum likelihood estimator under Gaussian noise. However, due to the constraint that
\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{U}=\bm{I}_{r}
, globally optimizing this function can be NP-hard in general.

We therefore propose to optimize the loss function
h
by projected gradient descent which constrains the current iterate
\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}
to be orthonormal at every iteration. Given an initialization
\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}
and stepsize
\eta
, we update
\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}
and
\bm{\widehat{R}}_{t}
via

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{R}}_{t}^{(l)}

\displaystyle:=\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{A}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t};\qquad l=1,\dots,L;

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+.5}

\displaystyle=\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}-\eta\nabla_{\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}}h\big(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t},\{\bm{\widehat{R}}^{(l)}_{t}\}_{l=1}^{L}\big);

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+1}

\displaystyle={\sf SVD}_{r}(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+.5}).

The gradient of
h
keeping
\bm{\widehat{R}}_{t}^{(l)}
fixed is given by

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}}h(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t},\{\bm{\widehat{R}}^{(l)}_{t}\})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\big(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{R}}_{t}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}-\bm{A}^{(l)}\big)\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{R}}_{t}^{(l)}.

(3)

For a given orthonormal
\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}
, the update
\bm{\widehat{R}}^{(l)}_{t}
is the closed-form minimizer of the objective function. Thus, the algorithm can be viewed as updating
\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}
via projected gradient descent, and then directly minimizing the error for each
\bm{\widehat{R}}_{t}^{(l)}
. Alternatively, this algorithm can be viewed as a Riemannian gradient descent algorithm on
\bm{\widehat{U}}
directly, where the SVD step acts as the retraction.

Warm Initialization via Spectral Sum of Squares

In order to initialize the algorithm, we propose using the sum of squares. Define

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}:={\sf SVD}_{r}\big(\sum_{l=1}^{L}(\bm{A}^{(l)})^{2}\big).

To understand this initialization, we note that squaring is in general necessary when the matrices
\bm{R}^{(l)}
are permitted to have negative eigenvalues. For example, in the simple case that

\displaystyle\bm{R}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&0\\
0&\lambda\end{pmatrix};\qquad\bm{R}^{(2)}=\begin{pmatrix}-\lambda&0\\
0&-\lambda\end{pmatrix},

it is straightforward to observe that simply averaging
\bm{A}^{(l)}
will result in a “canceling out” effect, resulting in pure noise, which is uninformative. More generally, if one has

\displaystyle\bm{R}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}a^{(l)}&b^{(l)}&b^{(l)}\\
b^{(l)}&a^{(l)}&b^{(l)}\\
b^{(l)}&b^{(l)}&a^{(l)}\end{pmatrix}

with some
a^{(l)}>b^{(l)}
and some
a^{(l)}<b^{(l)}
, then the average will exhibit a similar “canceling out” effect. Similar examples have appeared in previous works; notably
42
explicitly consider such settings in the context of multilayer stochastic blockmodels, which is a special case of the common subspace model.

When using the sum of squares, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg(\sum_{l}\big(\bm{A}^{(l)}\big)^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\big(\bm{S}^{(l)}\big)^{2}+\sigma^{2}L\frac{n+1}{2}\bm{I}_{n},

which has the property that its leading eigenspace is equal to
\bm{U}
(up to orthogonal transformation), since the addition of
\sigma^{2}L\frac{n+1}{2}\bm{I}_{n}
does not affect the eigenspace. The full algorithm is summarized in
Algorithm

1
.

Algorithm 1

Projected Gradient Descent Initialized via Spectral Sum of Squares

Collection of matrices
\{\bm{A}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{L}
; rank
r
, stepsize
\eta
, number of iterations
T
.

1.

Define

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}:={\sf SVD}_{r}\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{L}(\bm{A}^{(l)})^{2}\bigg).

(4)

2.

While
t<T
:

(a)

Set
\bm{\widehat{R}}^{(l)}_{t}:=\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{A}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}
.

(b)

Gradient step:

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+.5}:=\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}-\frac{\eta}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\big(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{R}}^{(l)}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}-\bm{A}^{(l)}\big)\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{R}}_{t}^{(l)}.

(5)

(c)

Projection step:

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+1}:={\sf SVD}_{r}(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+.5}).

(6)

\bm{\widehat{U}}_{T}
.

Estimation Upper and Lower Bounds

In this section we consider the statistical and computational limits for estimation.
We first have the following result.

Theorem 2

(Upper Bounds for Iterates)
.

Suppose that there exists some sufficiently large constant
C_{0}
such that

\displaystyle\lambda/\sigma\geq C_{0}\max\bigg\{\kappa\sqrt{r}\sqrt{\frac{nr}{L}},\kappa^{3}\sqrt{\frac{nr}{L}},\kappa r^{1/4}\frac{\sqrt{n}}{L^{1/4}},r\frac{\sqrt{n}}{L^{1/4}}\bigg\}.

(7)

Suppose each matrix
\bm{N}^{(l)}
is a subgaussian Wigner matrix with variance
\sigma^{2}
, and suppose also that
\max_{i,j,l}\|\bm{N}^{(l)}_{ij}\|_{\psi_{2}}\lesssim\sigma
and that
r\leq\min\{L,n\}
with
\log(L)\leq cnr
for some sufficiently small constant
c
, and suppose that
L\leq n^{2}
. Let
\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}
denote the output of
Algorithm

1
after
t
iterations, and suppose the stepsize
\eta
satisfies
\eta\in(\frac{c_{\eta}^{\prime}}{\lambda_{\max}^{2}},\frac{c_{\eta}}{\lambda_{\max}^{2}})
where
c_{\eta}^{\prime}<c_{\eta}<1
are any fixed constants. Then with probability at least
1-\exp(-cn)
, for all iterations
t
it holds that

\displaystyle\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t},\bm{U})\|_{F}\leq C_{1}\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{nr}}{\lambda\sqrt{L}}+\left(1-\frac{c_{\eta}^{\prime}}{8\kappa^{2}}\right)^{t}C_{2}\frac{\sigma^{2}n\sqrt{r}}{\sqrt{L}\lambda^{2}}.

Consequently, after
O\bigg(\log(\frac{\lambda/\sigma\sqrt{L}}{C_{1}\kappa\sqrt{nr}})\bigg)
iterations, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t},\bm{U})\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2C_{1}\sigma\kappa\sqrt{nr}}{\lambda\sqrt{L}}.

Proof.

See
Appendix

A
.
∎

The key assumption in
Theorem

2
is (
7
). When
\kappa,r\asymp 1
, its dominant requirement is that
\lambda/\sigma\gtrsim\sqrt{n}/L^{1/4}
. In
Section

3.1
, we argue that this condition is unavoidable for all polynomial-time estimators of
\bm{U}
.
A particularly interesting regime is when
\lambda/\sigma\ll\sqrt{n}
but
\lambda/\sigma\gtrsim\sqrt{n}/L^{1/4}
, since
\lambda/\sigma\ll\sqrt{n}
implies that individual subspace estimation cannot be consistent
(
22
)
. In this setting, the sum of squares spectral initialization (i.e.,
t=0
) incurs a “quadratic” error of order
\tfrac{\sigma^{2}n}{\lambda^{2}\sqrt{L}}
. This behavior mirrors subspace perturbation bounds for low-rank rectangular matrices of dimension
p_{1}\times p_{2}
with
p_{2}\gg p_{1}
, where a quadratic term dominates in the high-noise regime
(
22
;
13
)
.
This analogy is reinforced by the observation that
\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}
can be viewed as the matrix of left singular vectors of
[\bm{A}^{(1)},\ldots,\bm{A}^{(L)}]
, a rectangular matrix with
p_{1}=n
and
p_{2}=Ln
. However, unlike these other settings, the additional structure of the common subspace model allows projected gradient descent to reduce the effect of this quadratic error.

Several recent works have studied variants of the sum of squares spectral approach
(
42
;
68
;
75
;
72
;
3
)
. These papers focus on the heteroskedastic setting, where debiasing is necessary for improved estimation. In contrast, our model assumes homoskedastic noise, so no additional debiasing is required. Extending our analysis to heteroskedastic noise would be of both practical and theoretical interest, and we leave this direction for future work.

Statistical and Computational Lower Bounds

The purpose of this section is to demonstrate that the error and assumptions in
Theorem

2
are optimal. First, we provide a minimax lower bound for estimation with respect to the
\sin\Theta
loss. Define the parameter space

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\lambda,n,L,\sigma):=\bigg\{\{\bm{S}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{L}:\bm{S}^{(l)}=\bm{U}\bm{R}^{(l)}\bm{U}^{\top};\bm{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r},\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{U}=\bm{I}_{r},\lambda^{2}\leq\lambda_{\min}\bigg(\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l}(\bm{R}^{(l)})^{2}\bigg)\bigg\}.

(8)

We have the following minimax lower bound.

Theorem 3

(Minimax Lower Bound)
.

Assume that
r\leq c_{0}\sqrt{n}
for some sufficiently small constant
c_{0}
. Then it holds that

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{\widehat{U}}}\sup_{\mathcal{P}(\lambda,n,L,\sigma)}\mathbb{E}\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}},\bm{U})\|_{F}^{2}\gtrsim\frac{\sigma^{2}nr}{\lambda^{2}L}\wedge r,

where the infimum is taken over all estimators
\bm{\widehat{U}}
on the basis of the observation
\{\bm{A}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{L}
under
GOE
noise.

Proof.

See
Section

C.1
.
∎

Theorem

3
shows that the error bound attained by
Algorithm

1
in
Theorem

2
is minimax rate-optimal after logarithmically many iterations. However,
Theorem

3
only proves that a necessary condition for consistency is that
\lambda/\sigma\gg\sqrt{\frac{nr}{L}}
. In contrast,
Theorem

2
requires an additional condition, which, when
r,\kappa\asymp 1
is that
\lambda/\sigma\gtrsim\sqrt{n}/L^{1/4}
. We will provide evidence that this assumption is necessary for a polynomial-time estimator to exist using the low-degree likelihood ratio approach, which has provided evidence for a number of problems related to low-rank matrix estimation
(
55
;
43
;
50
)
. See
40
for a survey.

Explicitly, suppose one is given
L
i.i.d. observations
X_{1},\dots,X_{L}
, and consider testing the null hypothesis
H_{0}:X_{i}\sim\mathbb{Q}_{n}
versus the alternative
X_{i}\sim\mathbb{P}_{n}
. As is well-known, the likelihood ratio
L_{n}(\bm{X})=\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{n}(\bm{X})}{d\mathbb{Q}_{n}(\bm{X})}
is uniformly most powerful, and, furthermore, when
\mathbb{P}_{n}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{n}
are statistically indistinguishable (in the sense that the sum of Type I and Type II errors do not tend to zero) it holds that
\|L_{n}(\bm{X})\|^{2}=O(1)
as
n\to\infty
, where
\|\cdot\|^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}_{n}}(\cdot)^{2}
.
Building on this idea, in
40
the authors propose studying computational distinguishability by projecting
L_{n}(\bm{X})
onto polynomials of degree at most
D
. Define
L^{\leq D}_{n}(\bm{X})
as the orthogonal projection of
L_{n}(\bm{X})
onto polynomials of degree at most
D
.
40
conjecture that when
\|L^{\leq D}_{n}(\bm{X})\|^{2}=O(1)
, then the hypothesis test is
computationally
hard.

To apply the conjecture we consider the following two hypotheses:

\displaystyle H_{0}:

\displaystyle\ \bm{A}^{(l)}\sim\bm{N}^{(l)}\text{ for all $l$};

(9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}H_{a}:&\ \bm{A}^{(l)}\sim\bm{S}^{(l)}+\bm{N}^{(l)}\text{ for all $l$, where }\\
&\bm{S}^{(l)}=\varepsilon_{l}\lambda\bm{uu}^{\top};\\
&\varepsilon_{l}\sim\mathrm{IID\ Rademacher}.\end{split}

(10)

For simplicity we assume that
\bm{u}
has entries in
\pm n^{-1/2}
uniformly at random. This simple model is a rank-one version of the model we consider herein, where
\bm{u}
represents the shared subspace. The hypothesis test above tests pure noise against the alternative that there is a shared subspace
\bm{u}
. Our conjecture for this test is based on a variant from
55
.

Conjecture 1

(Conjecture 1 of
55
)
.

Let
\mathbb{Q}_{n}
denote the null hypothesis (
9
) and
\mathbb{P}_{n}
denote the alternative (
10
).
Denote
\bm{X}=\{\bm{A}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{L}
. If there exists
\varepsilon\geq 0
and
D\geq\log(nL)^{1+\varepsilon}
such that
\|L^{\leq D}_{n}(\bm{X})\|^{2}=1+o(1)
then there is no polynomial-time test
\phi_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{L\times n\times n}\mapsto\{0,1\}
such that the sum of Type I error and Type II error probabilities satisfy

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}_{n}}[\phi_{n}(\bm{X})]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{n}}[1-\phi_{n}(\bm{X})]\to 0

as
n\to\infty
.

Therefore, if
\|L_{n}^{\leq D}\|^{2}=1+o(1)
for this model,
1
suggests that there is no polynomial-time algorithm that can detect the existence of a rank one shared subspace. We have the following result.

Theorem 4

(Computational Lower Bound)
.

When
\lambda/\sigma=o\big(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{L^{1/4}}\big)
it holds that
\|L_{n}^{\leq D}\|^{2}=1+o(1)
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

See
Section

C.2
.
∎

Combining
Theorem

3
and
Theorem

4
, when
r,\kappa\asymp 1
, our proposed estimator attains minimax-optimal estimation error after logarithmically many iterations in the minimal regime such that a polynomial-time estimator exists. In particular, these results complete the statement of
Theorem

1
for the different Estimation SNR regimes.

Minimax-Optimal and Adaptive Inference

In this section we investigate the information-theoretical limits for statistical inference for the error
\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}},\bm{U})\|_{F}^{2}
, where we denote
\bm{\widehat{U}}=\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}
for
t
iterations. Define the matrix
\bm{\mathcal{R}}
via

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{R}}:=\big[\bm{R}^{(1)},\cdots,\bm{R}^{(L)}\big]\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times Lr}.

Our first result in this direction is a novel limit theorem in the Strong Inference SNR Regime.

Theorem 5

(Asymptotic Normality)
.

Suppose that the conditions of
Theorem

2
hold, and suppose
t\geq C_{\eta}\log\big(\frac{\kappa^{2}\lambda^{2}L}{\sigma^{2}nr}\big)
, where
C_{\eta}
depends on the step size
\eta
. Suppose further that
\lambda/\sigma\gg\frac{nr^{2}\kappa^{4}}{\sqrt{L}}
and
L\lesssim n
with
L\to\infty
. Suppose also that
\log(r)\lesssim\sqrt{L}
.
Assume that

\displaystyle\max\bigg\{\frac{\kappa^{4}}{L},\kappa^{4}\|\bm{U}\|_{2,\infty}^{2},\kappa^{2}\sqrt{nr}\|\bm{U}\|_{2,\infty}^{2},\frac{\kappa^{2}r^{3/2}\log(n)}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg\}\ll 1.

(11)

Then it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}},\bm{U})\|_{F}^{2}-\frac{\sigma^{2}n}{2}{\sf Tr}((\bm{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{R}}^{\top})^{-1})}{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n/2}\|(\bm{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{R}}^{\top})^{-1}\|_{F}}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1).

Proof.

See
Appendix

B
.
∎

We focus on the asymptotic regime where
L
grows with
n
, but still satisfies
L\lesssim n
. When
\kappa,r\asymp 1
, the condition
\|\bm{U}\|_{2,\infty}^{2}\ll 1/\sqrt{nr}
is quite mild, since
\|\bm{U}\|_{2,\infty}^{2}
can be as small as
\frac{r}{n}
. Therefore, the main restriction in
Theorem

5
is the SNR condition
\lambda/\sigma\gg\tfrac{nr^{2}\kappa^{4}}{\sqrt{L}},

which is much stronger than the condition
\lambda/\sigma\gtrsim\sqrt{n}/L^{1/4}
from
Theorem

2
when
\kappa,r\asymp 1
and
L\lesssim n
. Though this assumption may seem conservative, we will show in
Section

4.2
that it is actually unavoidable for adaptive inference.

Theorem

5
is closely related to several asymptotic normality results in the literature
(
65
;
66
;
11
)
. In particular,
66
proves asymptotic normality for the
\sin\Theta
distance in the matrix denoising model under Gaussian noise. Their theorem has a similar structure, except that the centering term depends not only on the singular values of the signal matrix, but also on the matrix dimensions
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
. This additional dependence comes from the dimension mismatch between
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
: when
d_{1}=d_{2}
, the centering term depends only on the singular values. In our setting, the singular values of
\bm{\mathcal{RR}}^{\top}
play an analogous role. A related result can also be found in
11
in the regime where the singular values are of order
\sqrt{n}
(after appropriate rescaling). Unlike
66
and
11
, we additionally provide estimators for the centering and scaling terms. Finally,
65
establishes a qualitatively similar result for tensor data, though our setting and proofs are different.

Remark 1

(Highlights of the Proof)
.

The proof of this result follows from
Theorem

9
, which establishes the first-order asymptotic expansion for
\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\|_{F}^{2}
.
The major first step of the proof is to show that

\displaystyle\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}

\displaystyle\approx\sum_{l}\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{U}\bm{R}^{(l)}(\bm{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{R}}^{\top})^{-1},

(12)

where the approximation holds up to small-order terms at least quadratic in the noise
\bm{N}^{(l)}
, together with a deterministic optimization error. To prove this approximation, we rely on decoupling arguments to control the quadratic error terms, as well as a novel argument showing that the loss function is geodesically convex (with respect to the Grassmannian manifold geometry) once all iterates are within the statistical neighborhood guaranteed by
Theorem

2
. After establishing this result, we then expand out the leading-order term to yield

\displaystyle\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle={\sf Tr}\bigg(\bm{U}_{\perp}\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bigg[\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{U}\bm{R}^{(l)}(\bm{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{R}}^{\top})^{-1}\bigg]\bigg[\sum_{l}(\bm{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{R}}^{\top})^{-1}\bm{R}^{(l)}\bm{U}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bigg]\bigg)+{\sf Res},

where
{\sf Res}
contains all the smaller-order terms and their interactions with the leading-order term. To handle this final residual term we separate it into terms of order three in
\bm{N}^{(l)}
and terms of order four and higher together with the deterministic optimization error. To handle the third-order terms, we appeal to decoupling concentration arguments for third-order polynomials of random variables together with
\varepsilon
-net arguments to handle the dependence. With this residual term controlled, our final result follows by showing that this leading-order term can be written as a martingale with respect to an appropriate filtration and applying a version of the Martingale Central Limit Theorem.

Rate Optimal Adaptive Confidence Intervals

Theorem

5
suggests that it is possible to derive confidence intervals via plug-in estimation. To determine whether these confidence intervals are minimax rate optimal, we first provide a minimax lower bound for the expected length of all
1-\alpha
honest confidence intervals. We will follow a similar framework as in
15
. To lay the groundwork, we will consider the parameter space
\mathcal{P}(\lambda,n,L,\sigma)
defined in (
8
). Since we are interested in the dependence on the SNR, we will use the shorthand
\mathcal{P}(\lambda)=\mathcal{P}(\lambda,n,L,\sigma)
, since changing
\sigma
is equivalent to changing
\lambda
by a rescaling argument.
Observe that
\mathcal{P}(\lambda_{1})\subset\mathcal{P}(\lambda_{2})
for any
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}
. Thus,
\mathcal{P}(\lambda)
forms a nested set of parameter spaces.

Given a parameter
0<\alpha<1
, and an estimate
\bm{\widehat{U}}
of
\bm{U}
and a parameter space
\mathcal{P}
, let
\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{P})
denote the set of all honest
1-\alpha
level confidence intervals for
\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}},\bm{U})\|_{F}^{2}
over
\mathcal{P}
; that is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{P}):

\displaystyle=\Bigg\{{\sf CI}_{\alpha}\big(\{\bm{A}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{L}\big)=[l,u]:\inf_{\theta\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\bigg(\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}},\bm{U})\|_{F}^{2}\in{\sf CI}_{\alpha}\big(\{\bm{A}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{L}\big)\bigg)\geq 1-\alpha\Bigg\}.

For simplicity we suppress the dependence of
{\sf CI}_{\alpha}
on the observations
\{\bm{A}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{L}
.
The length of the confidence interval
{\sf L}({\sf CI}_{\alpha})
is defined as the difference
u-l
. The maximum expected length over the parameter space
\mathcal{P}
is denoted as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{{\sf CI}_{\alpha}}(\mathcal{P}):=\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}{\sf L}\big({\sf CI}_{\alpha}\big).

Our first result gives a lower bound on the minimax expected length over
\mathcal{P}(\lambda)
.

Theorem 6
.

Suppose that
\lambda/\sigma\geq C_{0}\frac{nr}{\sqrt{L}}
for some sufficiently large constant
C_{0}>0
, and that
r\geq 2
.
Then there exists a constant
c^{\prime}>0
such that for all
n
sufficiently large,

\displaystyle\inf_{{\sf CI}_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{P}(\lambda))}\mathcal{L}_{{\sf CI}_{\alpha}}\big(\mathcal{P}(\lambda)\big)

\displaystyle\geq c^{\prime}\frac{\sigma^{2}nr}{\lambda^{2}L}\frac{1}{\sqrt{nr}}.

(13)

Proof.

See
Section

C.3
.
∎

When
\lambda/\sigma\geq C_{0}\frac{nr}{\sqrt{L}}
, we are in the regime where
Theorem

5
applies up to factors of
r
and
\kappa
. It is also straightforward to show that if
(\bm{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{R}}^{\top})^{-1}
is known, then the resulting confidence intervals will have the width given above. Thus, if it is possible to estimate the centering and scaling terms to appropriate fidelity, the resulting confidence intervals will have minimax optimal length.

We now discuss the construction of the optimal confidence interval that achieves the rate in
Theorem

6
. Define

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{\widehat{R}}}:=\begin{pmatrix}\bm{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\bm{A}^{(1)}\bm{\widehat{U}},\cdots,\bm{\widehat{U}}^{\top}\bm{A}^{(L)}\bm{\widehat{U}}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times rL}.

Let
z_{\alpha/2}
denote the
1-\alpha/2
quantile of the standard Gaussian distribution.
We define

\displaystyle\widehat{{\sf CI}_{\alpha}}:=\frac{\sigma^{2}n}{2}{\sf Tr}\big[\big(\bm{\mathcal{\widehat{R}}}\bm{\mathcal{\widehat{R}}}^{\top}\big)^{-1}\big]\pm z_{\alpha/2}\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n/2}\|\big(\bm{\mathcal{\widehat{R}}}\bm{\mathcal{\widehat{R}}}^{\top}\big)^{-1}\|_{F}.

This quantity can be understood as simple plug-in estimation of the centering and scaling terms in
Theorem

5
. Nonetheless, the following result shows that this confidence interval is optimal when
\bm{\widehat{U}}
is computed via
Algorithm

1
.

Theorem 7

(Confidence Interval Upper Bound)
.

Suppose the conditions of
Theorem

5
hold.
Then it holds that

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\inf_{\theta\in\mathcal{P}(\lambda)}\mathbb{P}\bigg(\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}},\bm{U})\|_{F}^{2}\in\widehat{{\sf CI}_{\alpha}}\bigg)\geq 1-\alpha,

and for all
n
sufficiently large, with probability at least
1-\exp(-cn)
,

\displaystyle{\sf L}(\widehat{{\sf CI}_{\alpha}})\lesssim\frac{\sigma^{2}nr}{\lambda^{2}L}\frac{1}{\sqrt{nr}}.

Proof.

See
Section

B.3
.
∎

Note that
Theorem

7
does not require sample splitting to achieve optimal inference, unlike other similar results in the literature on linear regression
(
16
;
15
)
and matrix completion
(
25
)
. Furthermore,
Theorem

7
demonstrates that when
\lambda/\sigma\gg\tfrac{n}{\sqrt{L}}
and
r,\kappa=O(1)
, our proposed confidence intervals are minimax-optimal. Since the construction of
\widehat{{\sf CI}_{\alpha}}
does not rely on knowledge of
\lambda

a priori
, the resulting confidence intervals are adaptive to signal strength.

Adaptivity Lower Bounds

In this section we determine whether adaptive confidence intervals can be constructed in the regime
\lambda/\sigma\lesssim\frac{n}{\sqrt{L}}
.
To study adaptivity, we consider a similar framework as in
15
. Given two nested parameter spaces
\mathcal{P}_{1}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}
, we define the adaptation benchmark

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}):=\inf_{{\sf CI}_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{P}_{2})}\mathcal{L}_{{\sf CI}_{\alpha}}(\mathcal{P}_{1}),

which is the infimum of the maximum expected length over
\mathcal{P}_{1}
among all
1-\alpha
confidence intervals over
\mathcal{P}_{2}
. Note that
Theorem

6
gives a lower bound on
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{P}(\lambda),\mathcal{P}(\lambda))
, and, moreover, the confidence intervals in
Theorem

7
attain this rate.

If a confidence interval is rate optimally adaptive over
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
, it should have optimal expected length simultaneously over both parameter spaces while still maintaining coverage over
\mathcal{P}_{2}
. Then this confidence interval must satisfy
\mathcal{L}_{{\sf CI}_{\alpha}}(\mathcal{P}_{1})\geq\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2})
. Hence, if
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2})
is significantly larger than the minimax rate over
\mathcal{P}_{1}
, then rate-optimal adaptation is impossible.

For technical reasons in the proof, we use the following slightly modified parameter space
\mathcal{\widetilde{P}}(\lambda)
defined via

\displaystyle\mathcal{\widetilde{P}}(\lambda):=\bigg\{\{\bm{S}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{L}:\bm{S}^{(l)}=\bm{U}\bm{R}^{(l)}\bm{U}^{\top};\bm{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r},\bm{U}^{\top}\bm{U}=\bm{I}_{r},r\lambda^{2}\leq\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\|\bm{R}^{(l)}\|_{F}^{2}\bigg\}.

If each
\bm{R}^{(l)}
is well-conditioned with condition number
O(1)
, then
\mathcal{\widetilde{P}}(\lambda)
coincides with
\mathcal{P}(\lambda)
up to constants. The following result shows the (lack of) adaptivity of confidence intervals over the two parameter spaces
\mathcal{\widetilde{P}}(\lambda_{1}),\mathcal{\widetilde{P}}(\lambda_{2})
.

Theorem 8
.

Take
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}
, where
\lambda_{1}/\sigma\geq\frac{nr}{\sqrt{L}}
. Suppose that
r\geq 2
. Then there exists some constant
c^{\prime}>0
such that for all
n
sufficiently large it holds that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\alpha}\big(\mathcal{\widetilde{P}}(\lambda_{1}),\mathcal{\widetilde{P}}(\lambda_{2})\big)\geq c^{\prime}\frac{\sigma^{2}nr}{\lambda_{1}^{2}L}\bigg(\frac{\sigma\sqrt{nr}}{\lambda_{2}\sqrt{L}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{nr}}\bigg).

Proof.

See
Section

C.3
.
∎

Observe that when
r,\kappa\asymp 1
, and
\lambda_{1}\gg\lambda_{2}
, with
\lambda_{2}/\sigma\ll\frac{nr}{\sqrt{L}}
, then rate-optimal adaptation is impossible.
However, if
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\gg\sigma\frac{nr}{\sqrt{L}}
, rate-optimal adaptation is possible, and this threshold matches the assumption in
Theorem

7
up to the factor of
r
.
Thus, considering
Theorems

6
,
8
and
7
, we have now established the existence of the Weak and Strong Inference SNR Regimes, which completes the proof of
Theorem

1
.

Related Work

Our work is closely connected to the literature on multilayer network analysis. A number of authors have considered estimation in the multilayer stochastic blockmodel with arbitrary connection probabilities, which can be viewed as a special case of the common subspace model
(
42
;
41
;
59
;
38
;
2
;
26
;
37
)
. Our proposed estimation procedure has previously been considered in
59
as “orthogonal-linked matrix factorization,” though statistical guarantees are only provided for global minima under a multilayer stochastic blockmodel. Our procedure does not actually require us to find a global minimum, and we show that our algorithm (with our initialization) is both statistically and computationally optimal.

The work
43
gives a computational lower bound for estimation in the multilayer stochastic blockmodel, but their results are sharp only up to logarithmic factors. In contrast, in this work we are able to explicitly characterize the computational limits in terms of the SNR. The works
55
;
53
are also closely related to this work, where they study the statistical and computational limits for a closely related model for mixtures of low-rank matrices. Our model is significantly more general than theirs, though their model can be viewed as a special case of the model we consider herein. In addition, none of the aforementioned works consider inference.

Our work is also related to the work
8
which proposes the common subspace model. They consider estimating
\bm{U}
by first setting
\bm{\widehat{U}}^{(l)}:={\sf SVD}_{r}(\bm{A}^{(l)})
and
\bm{\widehat{U}}={\sf SVD}_{r}([\bm{\widehat{U}}^{(1)},\bm{\widehat{U}}^{(2)},\cdots,\bm{\widehat{U}}^{(L)}])
. This procedure has been further studied and extended in several follow-on works
(
2
;
74
;
59
)
. However, a major deficiency of this procedure and its variants is that the first step requires each matrix
\bm{A}^{(l)}
to have sufficient signal strength. Under our statistical model, this is equivalent to requiring that
\lambda/\sigma\gg\sqrt{n}
, which does not benefit from the “aggregation effect” of having larger
L
.

In order to leverage the “aggregation” from multiple networks, the work
42
proposed to estimate
\bm{U}
(under a multilayer stochastic blockmodel) via a bias-adjusted sum of squares. Their procedure can essentially be viewed as a modified version of our initialization
\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0}
, where the modification consists of diagonal deletion due to the heteroskedasticity.
They prove an upper bound under a certain signal-strength condition (for Bernoulli noise), and this signal-strength condition was later shown to be computationally optimal in
43
. Subsequently,
68
proposed to further modify the procedure of
42
to “impute” the diagonal. The algorithm considered therein can be viewed as a Bernoulli-noise specific extension of the
{\sf HeteroPCA}
algorithm proposed in
72
and further studied in both
3
;
70
. However, in our work the focus is on optimal error rates and statistical inference under homoskedastic noise where bias adjustment is not necessary, and therefore the focus is significantly different.

Our work is also related to the literature on tensor data analysis, which exhibits similar statistical-computational gaps
(
73
;
51
;
9
;
52
;
7
)
, though our model is significantly different.
Our model is also related to several models considered in the data integration literature (e.g.,
48
). The works
56
;
71
;
46
are perhaps most similar, though only
56
consider the “weak-signal” regime we consider herein, though their focus is difrerent.

Turning to statistical inference, there have been a number of works on distributional theory and statistical inference for single networks or matrices
(
6
;
27
;
28
;
70
;
33
;
69
;
67
;
29
;
61
;
47
;
11
;
32
)
; see
1
for a survey in the stochastic blockmodel setting. However, inferential techniques for multilayer networks or collections of matrices are comparatively lacking, though there have been some efforts.
Our work is most closely related to the works
74
;
68
;
5
;
65
.
The work
74

considers the model herein and derives CLTs for the
rows
of the estimated subspace as well as the estimated score matrices. Similarly, in the work
68
the author develops entrywise limit theorems for the rows of the matrix
\bm{\widehat{U}}
obtained via bias-adjusted spectral sum of squares. Finally, the works
5
;
65
consider statistical inference for tensors. Our analyses are different from these prior works, and we primarily focus on the
\sin\Theta
distance. Of these, only
65
;
66
consider this distance, but neither of these papers consider the model herein, nor do they undertake a minimax analysis.

Our study on adaptive inference is inspired by a rich literature on adaptation theory in various settings. For example, our minimax result in
Theorem

8
is reminiscent of several results in high-dimensional sparse linear regression
(
16
;
15
;
23
;
14
;
34
;
58
)
and functional data analysis
(
18
;
17
;
19
;
62
;
36
;
20
;
44
;
45
;
21
)
. In these prior works the authors also consider adaptive confidence sets or confidence intervals, and they consider adaptation to an unknown parameter. However, the only result we know of considering adaptive inference in a matrix context is
25
, but the focus of that work is in adaptation with respect to the rank
r
, and they do not consider estimating subspaces. To the best of our knowledge, our study is the first to consider adaptive inference, where adaptation is considered with respect to the SNR.

Numerical Applications

In this section we apply
Algorithm

1
to both real and simulated data. In the subsequent section we consider simulated data, and in
Section

6.2
we consider an application to global trade data.

. Each color is a different value of

with

. The

-axis denotes the error averaged over 10 Monte Carlo repetitions.

n\in\{200,400\}
. For a given
L,\lambda
, and
n
, we first generate a subspace
\bm{U}
randomly by drawing a Gaussian random matrix of dimension
n\times r
, and then computing its left singular vectors. We then generate
\bm{R}^{(l)}
by drawing a random symmetric matrix of dimension
r\times r
with standard Gaussian entries, and then normalizing it so its smallest singular value is
\lambda
. Each
\bm{A}^{(l)}
is then drawn with
\sigma=1
according to the model (

).

and
\lambda=n^{c/16}
for

. Each different color is associated to a different value of
\lambda
. The dotted line corresponds to the spectral initialization (akin to the algorithm proposed in prior works), and the solid line denotes the error after 100 iterations of projected gradient descent. Notably, the error for a given value of
\lambda
is always smaller for projected gradient descent than the spectral initialization, but for sufficiently large
\lambda
, the two errors are much closer, and get smaller for larger
L
, which mirrors our theory directly.

(

averaged over 100 Monte Carlo repetitions.

Next, we consider confidence intervals for the error
\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t},\bm{U})\|_{F}^{2}
. We consider a similar setup as before, only now we consider
\lambda=n^{c/16}
with
c\in\{8,9,10,11\}
, since
Theorem

7
requires stronger signal strength. Table
1
shows the empirical probability that the true
\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}},\bm{U})\|_{F}^{2}
lies in the computed confidence interval with

, averaged over 100 samples for each

, with the left table associated to
n=200
and the right for
n=400
. According to our theory, as we increase
\lambda
and increase
L
, we expect the confidence intervals to be valid. Indeed, at
\lambda=n^{8/16}
we do not see validity for either
n
value, but for
\lambda=n^{9/16}
we see that the empirical probability is closer to .95 for larger
L
. For the row associated to
\lambda=n^{10/16}
, we again see that our resulting confidence intervals are approximately valid for all
L
. This phenomenon is reflected by both
n=200
and
n=400
.

Application to Trade Data

Dimensions 1 and 2.

Dimensions 3 and 4.

Figure 2
:
Resulting embedding of trade data

In this section we apply projected gradient descent with the spectral initialization to the trade data analyzed in several previous works
(
38
;
55
;
4
)
and collected by
30
. We preprocess the data as in
4
, resulting in
L=59
matrices of dimension
214\times 214
, where each matrix
\bm{A}^{(l)}
is a measure of the trading volume of commodity
l
between different countries. We run projected gradient descent with
r=5
as in
4
.

In
Fig.

2
we plot dimensions 1 and 2 (left) and dimensions 3 and 4 (right) of
\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}
. Observe that dimensions 1 and 2 have a clear distinction between Europe and Asia, and dimensions 3 and 4 have a clear separation of the USA from other countries. To further study the resulting embeddings, in
Fig.

3
we plot the percentile of each dimension, with largest values in red and smallest values in blue. In dimension 2 we see that most of Eastern Europe, Asia, and Africa are warmer colors, whereas Western Europe and North and South America are largely cooler colors. Dimension 4 contains similar behavior, except with the roles of Europe and the Americas reversed. This figure suggests that
Algorithm

1
is uncovering underlying shared structure.

:
Percentile of each column of
\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}
for each country, with warmer colors associated to larger values and cooler colors associated to smaller values.

Discussion

We have considered the statistical and computational limits for estimation of
\bm{U}
with respect to the Frobenius
\sin\Theta
distance, and the statistical limits for adaptive inference for the error
\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}},\bm{U})\|_{F}^{2}
. In practice, these results imply that statistical inference may be difficult without
a priori
knowledge of the signal strength, even when optimal estimation is feasible. There are several possible extensions, which we detail below.

•

Optimal dependence on
r
and
\kappa
.
In this work we have focused on the statistical and computational limits without optimizing for
r
and
\kappa
, and our results are somewhat loose with regard to these quantities. It would be of interest to determine the precise dependence on these quantities, and whether there are algorithms that are robust to overspecifying
r
, or that perform well with large values of
\kappa
.

•

Extension to heteroskedastic noise
. Our main results require, at the very least, that each
\bm{N}^{(l)}
is a Wigner matrix. However, in practice, the noise matrices may have heteroskedasticity, as is the case for binary network data. Extending our analysis to this setting may require additional modifications that explicitly account for heteroskedasticity.

•

Sharp phase transitions.
Our results demonstrate different phase transitions based on the order-wise behavior of
\lambda/\sigma
. It is of interest to determine the sharp constants for the error in all possible regimes of interest.

Acknowledgments

Part of this research was performed while the author was visiting the Institute for Mathematical and Statistical Innovation (IMSI), which is supported by the National Science Foundation (Grant No. DMS-1929348).

Appendix A
Proof of
Theorem

2

In this section we prove
Theorem

2
. Throughout our proofs we note that if an event holds with probability at least
1-C\exp(-cn)
then it holds with probability
1-\exp(-cn)
for some other constant
c
, and we do this replacement subsequently without further justification.

We will prove by induction. Our first result controls the spectral norm of the initialization error. Throughout our proofs, we let
\lambda_{\max}:=\max_{l}\|\bm{R}^{(l)}\|
.

Lemma 1
.

Under the conditions of
Theorem

2
, with probability at least
1-\exp(-cn)
it holds that

\displaystyle\bigg\|\sum_{l}\bigg((\bm{A}^{(l)})^{2}-(\bm{S}^{(l)})^{2}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}(n+1)\bm{I}_{n}\bigg)\bigg\|\lesssim\lambda_{\max}\sigma\sqrt{nL}+\sigma^{2}n\sqrt{L}.

Proof.

See
Section

A.1
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

2
: Initialization.

We now provide a bound for the spectral initialization. We will simply apply the Davis-Kahan Theorem. Observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{L}\mathbb{E}(\bm{A}^{(l)})^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}(\bm{S}^{(l)})^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}L(n+1)\bm{I}.

This shows that

\displaystyle\lambda_{r}\bigg(\sum_{l}(\bm{S}^{(l)})^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}(n+1)L\bm{I}\bigg)\geq L\lambda^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}nL.

Observe that by
Lemma

1
, with probability at least
1-\exp(-cn)
,

\displaystyle\bigg\|\sum_{l}\bigg(\big(\bm{A}^{(l)}\big)^{2}-\big(\bm{S}^{(l)}\big)^{2}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}(n+1)\bm{I}_{n}\bigg)\bigg\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda_{\max}\sigma\sqrt{nL}+\sigma^{2}n\sqrt{L}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L\lambda^{2}}{4}

where the final inequality holds whenever
\lambda/\sigma\geq C\max\big\{\kappa\sqrt{\frac{n}{L}},\frac{\sqrt{n}}{L^{1/4}}\big\}
which holds when (
7
) is satisfied. Therefore, by the Davis-Kahan Theorem, we have that with this same probability,

\displaystyle\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0},\bm{U})\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\lambda_{\max}\sigma\sqrt{nL}+\sigma^{2}n\sqrt{L}}{L\lambda^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\kappa\sigma\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{L}\lambda}+\frac{\sigma^{2}n}{\lambda^{2}\sqrt{L}}.

Using the deterministic inequality
\|\bm{A}\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{r}\|\bm{A}\|
for any matrix
\bm{A}
of rank at most
r
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{0},\bm{U})\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\kappa\sigma\sqrt{nr}}{\sqrt{L}\lambda}+\frac{C\sigma^{2}n\sqrt{r}}{\lambda^{2}\sqrt{L}}

whenever the event in
Lemma

1
holds.
∎

We prove
Theorem

2
by induction. For simplicity we only consider the regime where
\sqrt{n}\gtrsim\lambda/\sigma\gtrsim\sqrt{n}/L^{1/4}
since otherwise no additional iterations are required and the result holds for
t=0
. We also do not consider the updates for
\bm{\widehat{R}}^{(l)}_{t}
since we can encode these updates directly into the algorithm, since
\bm{\widehat{R}}^{(l)}_{t}=\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{A}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}
. Therefore, the gradient updates are equivalently given by

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+1/2}

\displaystyle=\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}-\frac{\eta}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\big(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{R}}^{(l)}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}-\bm{A}^{(l)}\big)\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{R}}_{t}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}-\frac{\eta}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\big(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{A}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}-\bm{A}^{(l)}\big)\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{A}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t};

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+1}

\displaystyle={\sf SVD}_{r}(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+1/2}).

This algorithm does not include any updates for
\bm{\widehat{R}}^{(l)}_{t}
, and it is equivalent to
Algorithm

1
.

Define the event

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{{\sf good}}

\displaystyle:=\bigg\{\bigg\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{Q}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bigg\|\leq C\sigma\sqrt{rnL}\lambda_{\max}\|\bm{Q}\|_{F}\text{ for all matrices $\bm{Q}$ of rank at most $2r$}\bigg\}

(14a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\bigcap\bigg\{\bigg\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{Q}\bm{N}^{(l)}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}L\big(\bm{Q}^{\top}+{\sf Tr}(\bm{Q})\bm{I}\big)\bigg\|\leq C\sigma^{2}nr\sqrt{L}\|\bm{Q}\|_{F}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{ for all matrices $\bm{Q}$ of rank at most $2r$}\bigg\}\end{split}

(14b)

\displaystyle\bigcap\bigg\{\bigg\|\sum_{l}\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{UU}^{\top}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{U}\bigg\|\leq C\sigma^{2}r\max\{\sqrt{nL},n\}\bigg\}

(14c)

\displaystyle\bigcap\bigg\{\bigg\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bigg\|\leq C\sigma\sqrt{nL}\lambda_{\max}\bigg\}

(14d)

We will prove the result by induction on
t
on the event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf good}}
. The following lemma shows that
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf good}}
holds with high probability.

Lemma 2
.

Suppose that each
\bm{N}^{(l)}
is a subgaussian Wigner matrix with variance
\sigma^{2}
and
\psi_{2}
norm bounded by
\sigma
. Suppose that
\max_{l}\|\bm{S}^{(l)}\|\leq\lambda_{\max}
and
\log(L)\leq cnr
for some sufficiently small constant
c
. Then the event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf good}}
holds with probability at least
1-\exp(-cn)
.

Proof.

See
Section

A.2
.
∎

To give the proof we will require some additional bounds on the event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf good}}
. Define

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\sf L}_{t}&:=-\frac{1}{L}\big(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}-\bm{UU}^{\top}\big)\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}+\frac{1}{L}\bm{U}_{\perp}\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\\
&\quad-\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}+\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\end{split}

(15)

\displaystyle{\sf Q}_{t}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{L}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}+\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t},

(16)

which are the terms linear and quadratic in
\bm{N}^{(l)}
respectively.
The following lemma controls the linear error.

Lemma 3
.

Suppose the conditions of
Theorem

2
hold.
Suppose that

\displaystyle\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\|_{F}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Then on the event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf good}}
it holds that

\displaystyle\|\bm{U}_{\perp}\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}{\sf L}_{t}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\|_{F}\frac{\sigma\lambda_{\max}\sqrt{nr}}{\sqrt{L}}+C\frac{\sigma\lambda_{\max}\sqrt{nr}}{\sqrt{L}};

\displaystyle\|{\sf L}_{t}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{r}\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\|_{F}\frac{\sigma\lambda_{\max}\sqrt{nr}}{\sqrt{L}}+C\frac{\sigma\lambda_{\max}\sqrt{nr}}{\sqrt{L}}.

Proof.

See
Section

A.3
.
∎

The following lemma bounds the term
{\sf Q}_{t}
.

Lemma 4
.

Suppose the conditions of
Theorem

2
hold.
Suppose that

\displaystyle\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\|_{F}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Then on the event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf good}}
it holds that

\displaystyle\|\bm{U}_{\perp}\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}{\sf Q}_{t}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\|_{F}\frac{C\sigma^{2}nr^{3/2}}{\sqrt{L}}+\frac{C\sigma^{2}r^{3/2}\max\{\sqrt{nL},n\}}{L}

\displaystyle\|{\sf Q}_{t}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\sigma^{2}nr^{2}}{\sqrt{L}}

Proof.

See
Section

A.4
.
∎

Finally, the following technical lemma is needed to ensure that the iterates contract.

Lemma 5
.

Suppose the conditions of
Theorem

2
hold.
Suppose that

\displaystyle\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\|_{F}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Then on the event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf good}}
it holds that

\displaystyle\lambda_{r}\bigg(\sum_{l}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{L\lambda^{2}}{4};

\displaystyle\|\sum_{l}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\|

\displaystyle\leq L\lambda_{\max}^{2}.

Proof.

See
Section

A.5
.
∎

With these lemmas in place we now have the proof of
Theorem

2
for
t\geq 1
.

Proof of
Theorem

2
: Induction Step.

Define the event
\mathcal{E}_{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:initialconcentration}}}
as the event that

\displaystyle\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}}^{(0)},\bm{U})\|_{F}\leq\underbrace{C_{1}\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{nr}}{\lambda\sqrt{L}}}_{=:A}+\underbrace{C_{2}\frac{\sigma^{2}n\sqrt{r}}{\sqrt{L}\lambda^{2}}}_{=:B}.

Throughout we condition on the event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf good}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:initialconcentration}}}
.
By
Lemma

1
,
\mathcal{E}_{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:initialconcentration}}}
holds with probability at least
1-\exp(-cn)
, and by
Lemma

2
,
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf good}}
holds with this same probability.
Under (
7
) and the event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf good}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:initialconcentration}}}
, it holds that
\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\|_{F}\leq\frac{1}{2}

provided
C_{0}
is sufficiently large. Therefore, suppose that at time
t
it holds that

\displaystyle\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t},\bm{U})\|_{F}\leq A+\big(1-\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{8}\big)^{t}B.

Clearly this holds at time
t=0
.
The gradient update gives

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+1/2}

\displaystyle=\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}-\frac{\eta}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\bigg(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{A}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}-\bm{A}^{(l)}\bigg)\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{A}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}-\frac{\eta}{L}\big(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}-\bm{UU}^{\top}\big)\sum_{l=1}^{L}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\big(\bm{S}^{(l)}\big)\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\eta}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\bigg(\big(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}-\bm{U}\bm{U}^{\top}\big)\bm{N}^{(l)}-\bm{U}_{\perp}\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bigg)\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\big(\bm{S}^{(l)}\big)\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\eta}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\big(\bm{S}^{(l)}+\bm{N}^{(l)}\big)\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\eta}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\big(\bm{S}^{(l)}+\bm{N}^{(l)}\big)\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\big(\bm{N}^{(l)}\big)\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}.

From the definitions of
{\sf L}_{t}
and
{\sf Q}_{t}
in
Eq.

15
and
Eq.

16
it holds that

\displaystyle\bm{U}_{\perp}\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+1/2}

\displaystyle=\bm{U}_{\perp}\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}-\frac{\eta}{L}\bm{U}_{\perp}\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\sum_{l}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}+\eta\bm{U}_{\perp}\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bigg({\sf L}_{t}+{\sf Q}_{t}\bigg).

From
Lemma

3
and
Lemma

4
we have that

\displaystyle\|\bm{U}_{\perp}\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}{\sf L}_{t}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\|_{F}\frac{\sigma\sqrt{nr}\lambda_{\max}}{\sqrt{L}}+C\frac{\sigma\lambda_{\max}\sqrt{nr}}{\sqrt{L}};

\displaystyle\|\bm{U}_{\perp}\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}{\sf Q}_{t}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\|_{F}\frac{C\sigma^{2}nr^{3/2}}{\sqrt{L}}+\frac{C\sigma^{2}r^{3/2}\max\{\sqrt{nL},n\}}{L}.

By
Lemma

5
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{r}\left(\frac{\eta}{L}\sum_{l}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\right)\geq\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{4}.

Since
\eta\lambda^{2}\leq 1
, this yields

\displaystyle\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+1/2}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\|_{F}\left(1-\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{4}+\eta\frac{C\sigma\sqrt{nr}\lambda_{\max}}{\sqrt{L}}+\eta\frac{C\sigma^{2}nr^{3/2}}{\sqrt{L}}\right)+\eta\frac{C\sigma\lambda_{\max}\sqrt{nr}}{\sqrt{L}}+\eta\frac{C\sigma^{2}r^{3/2}\max\{\sqrt{nL},n\}}{L}.

Under (
7
), since
C_{1}
is fixed, it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{C\sigma\lambda_{\max}\sqrt{nr}}{\sqrt{L}}+\frac{C\sigma^{2}r^{3/2}\max\{\sqrt{nL},n\}}{L}\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}}{64}A.

Therefore, from the induction hypothesis,

\displaystyle\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+1/2}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(1-\frac{7\eta\lambda^{2}}{32}\bigg)\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\|_{F}+\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{64}A

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(1-\frac{7\eta\lambda^{2}}{32}\bigg)\bigg(A+\big(1-\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{8}\big)^{t}B\bigg)+\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{64}A

\displaystyle=A\left(1-\frac{13\eta\lambda^{2}}{64}\right)+\left(1-\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{8}\right)^{t}B\left(1-\frac{7\eta\lambda^{2}}{32}\right).

We next bound the projection step. Write

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+1/2}=\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+1}\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{V}}_{t}^{\top}.

Then

\displaystyle\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+1},\bm{U})\|_{F}\leq\|\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{t}^{-1}\|\,\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+1/2}\|_{F}.

We claim that

\displaystyle\|\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{t}^{-1}\|\leq 1+\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{32}.

Indeed, write

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+1/2}=\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}+\mathcal{I}_{t},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{t}:=-\frac{\eta}{L}\big(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}-\bm{U}\bm{U}^{\top}\big)\sum_{l}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}+\eta{\sf L}_{t}+\eta{\sf Q}_{t}.

Using
\eta\lambda_{\max}^{2}\leq c_{\eta}
and the induction hypothesis,

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\eta}{L}\big(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}-\bm{U}\bm{U}^{\top}\big)\sum_{l}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta\lambda_{\max}^{2}\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq 2c_{\eta}\left[A+\left(1-\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{8}\right)^{t}B\right].

Since
\eta\lambda^{2}\geq\frac{c_{\eta}^{\prime}}{\kappa^{2}},

(
7
) implies that
A+B\leq\frac{c_{\eta}^{\prime}}{1024c_{\eta}}\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}
and hence

\displaystyle\big\|\frac{\eta}{L}\big(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}-\bm{U}\bm{U}^{\top}\big)\sum_{l}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\big\|

\displaystyle\leq 2c_{\eta}(A+B)

\displaystyle\leq 2c_{\eta}\frac{c_{\eta}^{\prime}}{1024c_{\eta}}\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{512}.

Similarly, by
Lemmas

3
and
4
and (
7
),

\displaystyle\|\eta{\sf L}_{t}\|_{F}+\|\eta{\sf Q}_{t}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\eta\|\bm{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t}\|_{F}\frac{C\sigma\sqrt{n}r\lambda_{\max}}{\sqrt{L}}+\eta\frac{C\sigma\lambda_{\max}\sqrt{nr}}{\sqrt{L}}+\eta\frac{C\sigma^{2}nr^{2}}{\sqrt{L}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{512}.

Hence, it holds that
\|\mathcal{I}_{t}\|\leq\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{256}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\|\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+1/2}^{\top}\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+1/2}-\bm{I}_{r}\right\|\leq 2\|\mathcal{I}_{t}\|+\|\mathcal{I}_{t}\|^{2}\leq\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{128}+\frac{\eta^{2}\lambda^{4}}{256^{2}}\leq\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{64}.

By Weyl’s inequality,
\lambda_{r}^{2}(\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{t})\geq 1-\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{64}.

Thus, since
\eta\lambda^{2}\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\|\bm{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{t}^{-1}\|\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\eta\lambda^{2}/64}}\leq 1+\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{32}.

Therefore, we have shown that

\displaystyle\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t+1},\bm{U})\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{32}\right)\left[A\left(1-\frac{13\eta\lambda^{2}}{64}\right)+\left(1-\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{8}\right)^{t}B\left(1-\frac{7\eta\lambda^{2}}{32}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq A+\left(1-\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{8}\right)^{t+1}B.

Hence, for every
t\geq 0
, on the event
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf good}}
,

\displaystyle\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t},\bm{U})\|_{F}\leq C_{1}\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{nr}}{\lambda\sqrt{L}}+\left(1-\frac{\eta\lambda^{2}}{8}\right)^{t}C_{2}\frac{\sigma^{2}n\sqrt{r}}{\sqrt{L}\lambda^{2}}.

Since
\eta\lambda^{2}>c_{\eta}^{\prime}/\kappa^{2}
, we also have

\displaystyle\|\sin\Theta(\bm{\widehat{U}}_{t},\bm{U})\|_{F}\leq C_{1}\frac{\sigma\kappa\sqrt{nr}}{\lambda\sqrt{L}}+\left(1-\frac{c_{\eta}^{\prime}}{8\kappa^{2}}\right)^{t}C_{2}\frac{\sigma^{2}n\sqrt{r}}{\sqrt{L}\lambda^{2}}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

1

Proof.

Without loss of generality we let
\sigma^{2}=1
.
We define

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle:=\bigg\|\sum_{l}\bigg((\bm{A}^{(l)})^{2}-(\bm{S}^{(l)})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(n+1)\bm{I}_{n}\bigg)\bigg\|.

It holds that
S\leq S_{L}+S_{Q}
where
S_{L}
and
S_{Q}
are defined via

\displaystyle S_{L}:

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\sum_{l}\bigg(\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{S}^{(l)}+\bm{S}^{(l)}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bigg)\bigg\|;

\displaystyle S_{Q}:

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\sum_{l}\bigg((\bm{N}^{(l)})^{2}-\mathbb{E}\big(\bm{N}^{(l)}\big)^{2}\bigg)\bigg\|.

We will bound each term separately.

Bounding
S_{L}
:
First, we note that

\displaystyle\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{S}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\mathcal{P}_{{\sf upper-diag}}(\bm{N}^{(l)})\bm{S}^{(l)}+\sum_{l}\mathcal{P}_{{\sf upper-diag}}^{c}(\bm{N}^{(l)})\bm{S}^{(l)},

where
\mathcal{P}_{{\sf upper-diag}}
is the operator that sets its lower off-diagonal elements to zero. Without loss of generality we bound the first term; the second is similar.

Let
x
be any deterministic unit vector in the span of
\bm{UU}^{\top}
, and let
y
be any deterministic
n
-dimensional unit vector. Then we can write

\displaystyle y^{\top}\sum_{l}\mathcal{P}_{{\sf upper-diag}}(\bm{N}^{(l)})\bm{S}^{(l)}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{l}y_{i}\mathcal{P}_{{\sf upper-diag}}(\bm{N}^{(l)})_{ij}\bm{S}^{(l)}_{jk}x_{k}.

Note that for fixed
x
and
y
this is a sum of independent subgaussian random variables. We will note that its
\psi_{2}
norm can be bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq n}\sum_{l}y_{i}^{2}\bigg(\sum_{k}\bm{S}^{(l)}_{jk}x_{k}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\sum_{j}\|e_{j}^{\top}\bm{S}^{(l)}x\|^{2}=\sum_{l}\|\bm{S}^{(l)}x\|^{2}\leq L\lambda_{\max}^{2},

since
\sup_{\|x\|=1}\|\bm{S}^{(l)}x\|^{2}\leq\lambda_{\max}^{2}
. By taking a union bound via
\varepsilon
-net, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{S}^{(l)}\|\leq Ct

with probability at least
1-\exp\bigg(cn-\frac{t^{2}}{L\lambda_{\max}^{2}}\bigg).

Consequently, letting
\frac{t^{2}}{L\lambda_{\max}^{2}}=s^{2}
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{S}^{(l)}\|\leq Cs\sqrt{L}\lambda_{\max}

with probability at least
1-\exp\bigg(cn-s^{2}\bigg).
Therefore, we may let
s=C\sqrt{n}
to obtain that

\displaystyle\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{S}^{(l)}\|\leq C\sqrt{nL}\lambda_{\max}

with probability at least
1-\exp(-cn).

Bounding
S_{Q}
:
First, we have

\displaystyle S_{Q}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\sum_{l}(\bm{N}^{(l)})^{2}-\mathbb{E}(\bm{N}^{(l)})^{2}\bigg\|\leq\bigg\|\sum_{l}\mathcal{H}(\bm{N}^{(l)})^{2}\bigg\|+\bigg\|\sum_{l}\mathcal{D}(\bm{N}^{(l)})^{2}-\mathbb{E}(\bm{N}^{(l)})^{2}\bigg\|,

where
\mathcal{H}(\cdot)
is the
hollowing operator
that sets the diagonal to zero, and
\mathcal{D}
is the
diagonal operator
that sets the off-diagonal to zero. We bound each term in turn.

•

The off-diagonal term.

Observe that the first term can be written as a U-statistic in the entries of
\bm{N}^{(l)}
, and we may apply Theorem 1 of
60
to obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\sum_{l}\mathcal{H}(\bm{N}^{(l)})^{2}\bigg\|>t\bigg\}\leq C\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\bigg\|\sum_{l}\mathcal{H}\bigg(\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{(l)}\bigg)\bigg\|>Ct\bigg\},

where
\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{(l)}
is an independent copy. We then have that

\displaystyle\bigg\|\sum_{l}\mathcal{H}\bigg(\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{(l)}\bigg)\bigg\|\leq\underbrace{\bigg\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{(l)}\bigg\|}_{=:T_{1}}+\underbrace{\bigg\|\sum_{l}\mathcal{D}\bigg(\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{(l)}\bigg)\bigg\|}_{=:T_{2}}.

We now bound each term
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
as follows.

–

The term
T_{2}
. For the diagonal term above, we note that the
i
’th diagonal entry can be written as

\displaystyle\sum_{l}\sum_{j}\bm{N}^{(l)}_{ij}\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{(l)}_{ij}.

Conditional on
\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{(l)}
, the above is a subgaussian random variable with variance proxy
\sum_{l}\sum_{j}\big(\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{(l)}_{ij}\big)^{2}
. Therefore, it can be bounded via

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{l}\sum_{j}\bm{N}^{(l)}_{ij}\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{(l)}_{ij}\bigg|\leq Ct\sqrt{\sum_{l}\sum_{j}\big(\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{(l)}_{ij}\big)^{2}}

with probability at least
1-2\exp(-ct^{2})
, conditional on
\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{(l)}
. Using concentration of the norm for subgaussian random variables, uniformly over
i
it holds that

\displaystyle\sqrt{\sum_{l}\sum_{j}\big(\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{(l)}_{ij}\big)^{2}}\leq C\sqrt{nL}

(17)

with probability at least
1-2n\exp(-cnL)
. Therefore, on this event, for all
i
, with probability at least
1-2n\exp(-ct^{2})
it holds that

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{l}\sum_{j}\bm{N}^{(l)}_{ij}\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{(l)}_{ij}\bigg|\leq Ct\sqrt{nL},

whenever the event (
17
) holds. Combining it all we have that this quantity is bounded by
Cn\sqrt{L}
with probability at least
1-2n\exp(-cn)-2n\exp(-cnL)
.

–

The term
T_{1}
.
We replicate our proof for
\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{S}^{(l)}\|
only replacing
\bm{S}^{(l)}
with
\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{(l)}
. Conditional on the event

\displaystyle\sup_{\|x\|=1}\sum_{l}\|\bm{N}^{(l)}x\|^{2}\leq CLn,

it holds that

\displaystyle\bigg\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{\widetilde{N}}^{(l)}\bigg\|\leq Cs\sqrt{Ln}

with probability at least
1-2\exp(cn-s^{2})
. Taking
s\asymp\sqrt{n}
yields the same bound, which holds with probability at least
1-2\exp(-cn)-2\exp(-cLn)
.

We have thus shown that

\displaystyle\bigg\|\sum_{l}\mathcal{H}\bigg((\bm{N}^{(l)})^{2}\bigg)\bigg\|\leq Cn\sqrt{L}

with probability at least
1-8n\exp(-cn)
.

•

The Diagonal Term
. We observe that

\displaystyle\bigg\|\mathcal{D}\bigg(\sum_{l}(\bm{N}^{(l)})^{2}-\mathbb{E}(\bm{N}^{(l)})^{2}\bigg)\bigg\|

\displaystyle=\max_{i}\bigg|\sum_{l,j}(\bm{N}^{(l)}_{ij})^{2}-\mathbb{E}(\bm{N}^{(l)}_{ij})^{2}\bigg|.

For fixed
i
, this is a subexponential random variable. Bernstein’s inequality implies that

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{l,j}(\bm{N}^{(l)}_{ij})^{2}-\mathbb{E}(\bm{N}^{(l)}_{ij})^{2}\bigg|\leq t

with probability at least
1-2\exp(-c\min\{\frac{t^{2}}{Ln},t\})
. Take
t=Cn\sqrt{L}
and a union bound to show that

\displaystyle\bigg\|\mathcal{D}\bigg(\sum_{l}(\bm{N}^{(l)})^{2}-\mathbb{E}(\bm{N}^{(l)})^{2}\bigg)\bigg\|\lesssim n\sqrt{L}

with probability at least
1-2n\exp(-cn)
.

Combining all these inequalities and adjusting constants completes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

2

Proof of
Lemma

2
.

The proof for (
14a
) is similar to the proof of
Lemma

1
. First, let
\bm{Q}
be any fixed matrix of rank at most
2r
. Slightly modifying the proof of
Lemma

1
shows that we have that for any fixed matrix
\bm{Q}
it holds that

\displaystyle\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{Q}\bm{S}^{(l)}\|\lesssim C\sigma s\sqrt{L}\lambda_{\max}\|\bm{Q}\|_{F}

with probability at least
1-\exp(cn-s^{2})
. Next, consider

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{Q}}:=\argsup_{\bm{Q}:\|\bm{Q}\|_{F}\leq 1;\text{rank}(\bm{Q})\leq 2r}\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{Q}\bm{S}^{(l)}\|.

Let
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}
be an
\varepsilon
-net for matrices of rank at most
2r
with Frobenius norm at most 1. Then
|\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}|\leq\exp(cnr)
by Lemma 3.1 of
24
. Define the event

\displaystyle\sup_{\bm{Q}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}}\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{Q}\bm{S}^{(l)}\|\leq C\sigma\sqrt{Lnr}\lambda_{\max}.

(18)

By a union bound (
18
) holds with probability at least
1-\exp(-cnr)
for an appropriate choice of
C
above. Let
\bm{Q}_{\varepsilon}
be the matrix in
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}
within
\varepsilon
of
\bm{\widetilde{Q}}
. Without loss of generality we can assume that
(\bm{\widetilde{Q}}-\bm{Q}_{\varepsilon})\bm{U}\neq 0
(since if it does the result is zero). In this case, note that

\displaystyle\|\big(\bm{\widetilde{Q}}-\bm{Q}_{\varepsilon}\big)\bm{UU}^{\top}\|_{F}\leq\varepsilon<1,

and hence
\big(\bm{\widetilde{Q}}-\bm{Q}_{\varepsilon}\big)\bm{UU}^{\top}
is a matrix of rank at most
2r
with Frobenius norm at most 1. Furthermore, when (
18
) holds,

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle:=\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{\widetilde{Q}}\bm{S}^{(l)}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\big(\bm{\widetilde{Q}}-\bm{Q}_{\varepsilon}\big)\bm{S}^{(l)}\|+\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\big(\bm{Q}_{\varepsilon}\big)\bm{S}^{(l)}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\big(\bm{\widetilde{Q}}-\bm{Q}_{\varepsilon}\big)\bm{UU}^{\top}\|_{F}\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\frac{\big(\bm{\widetilde{Q}}-\bm{Q}_{\varepsilon}\big)}{\|\big(\bm{\widetilde{Q}}-\bm{Q}_{\varepsilon}\big)\bm{UU}^{\top}\|_{F}}\bm{S}^{(l)}\|+\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\big(\bm{Q}_{\varepsilon}\big)\bm{S}^{(l)}\|

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon M+C\sigma\sqrt{Lnr}\lambda_{\max}.

This implies that
M\lesssim\sqrt{Lnr}\lambda_{\max}
with probability at least
1-\exp(-cnr)
, which is the assertion (
14a
).

To prove (
14b
), we similarly apply an
\varepsilon
-net, but we also use a decoupling argument. First, fix any matrix
\bm{Q}
of rank at most
2r
. Observe that since each
\bm{N}^{(l)}
is a Wigner matrix, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{Q}\bm{N}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\bigg(\bm{Q}^{\top}+{\sf diag}\big({\sf Tr}(\bm{Q})\big)\bigg).

Consequently, we have that

\displaystyle\bigg\|\sum_{l}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{Q}\bm{N}^{(l)}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}L\big(\bm{Q}^{\top}+{\sf Tr}(\bm{Q})\bm{I}\big)\bigg\|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\|\sum_{l}\mathcal{H}\bigg(\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{Q}\bm{N}^{(l)}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\bm{Q}^{\top}\bigg)\bigg\|

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg\|\sum_{l}\mathcal{D}\bigg(\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{Q}\bm{N}^{(l)}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\big(\bm{Q}+{\sf Tr}(\bm{Q})\bm{I}\big)\bigg)\bigg\|,

where we have separated the off-diagonal from the diagonal. We will bound each term separately for fixed
\bm{Q}
.

•

The off-diagonal.
We have that the
i,i^{\prime}
entry is given by (for
i\neq i^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle\bigg(\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{Q}\bm{N}^{(l)}\bigg)_{ii^{\prime}}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\bm{Q}_{i^{\prime}i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\bm{N}^{(l)}_{ij}\bm{Q}_{jk}\bm{N}^{(l)}_{i^{\prime}k}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\bm{Q}_{i^{\prime}i}.

Let
\bm{x,y}
be unit vectors. Then we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{l}\bm{x}^{\top}\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle\bigg(\bm{N}^{(l)}\bm{Q}\bm{N}^{(l)}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\bm{Q}^{\top}\bigg)\bm{y}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\bigg(\sum_{i\neq i^{\prime}}\sum_{j,k}\bm{x}_{i}\bm{y}_{i^{\prime}}\bm{N}^{(l)}_{ij}\bm{Q}_{jk}\bm{N}^{(l)}_{i^{\prime}k}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\bm{x}^{\top}\mathcal{H}(\bm{Q}^{\top})\bm{y}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\bigg(\sum_{i\neq i^{\prime}}\bm{x}_{i}\bm{y}_{i^{\prime}}\bm{N}^{(l)}_{ii^{\prime}}\bm{Q}_{i^{\prime}i}\bm{N}^{(l)}_{i^{\prime}i}+\sum_{i\neq i^{\prime}}\sum_{j\neq i^{\prime},k\neq i}\bm{x}_{i}\bm{y}_{i^{\prime}}\bm{N}^{(l)}_{ij}\bm{Q}_{jk}\bm{N}^{(l)}_{i^{\prime}k}-\bm{x}^{\top}\mathcal{H}(\bm{Q}^{\top})\bm{y}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\sum_{i<i^{\prime}}\big[\big(\bm{N}^{(l)}_{ii^{\prime}}\big)^{2}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\big]\bigg(\bm{x}_{i}\bm{y}_{i^{\prime}}\bm{Q}_{i^{\prime}i}+\bm{x}_{i^{\prime}}\bm{y}_{i}\bm{Q}_{ii^{\prime}}\bigg)+\sum_{l}\sum_{i\neq i^{\prime}}\sum_{j\neq i^{\prime},k\neq i}\bm{x}_{i}\bm{y}_{i^{\prime}}\bm{N}^{(l)}_{ij}\bm{Q}_{jk}\bm{N}^{(l)}_{i^{\prime}k}.

We will control each of these two terms separately.

–

The first term
: Observe that the first term is a sum of independent mean-zero sub-exponential random variables. We have that

\displaystyle\sum_{l}\sum_{i<i^{\prime}}\bigg\|\big[\big(\bm{N}^{(l)}_{ii^{\prime}}\big)^{2}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\big]\bigg(\bm{x}_{i}\bm{y}_{i^{\prime}}\bm{Q}_{ii^{\prime}}+\bm{x}_{i^{\prime}}\bm{y}_{i}\bm{Q}_{i^{\prime}i}\bigg)\bigg\|_{\psi_{1}}^{2}\leq CL\sigma^{4}\sum_{i,i^{\prime}}\big(\bm{x}_{i}\bm{y}_{i^{\prime}}\bm{Q}_{i^{\prime}i}\big)^{2}\lesssim\sigma^{4}L\|\bm{Q}\|^{2},

since
\bm{x}
and
\bm{y}
are unit vectors. Similarly, we have that
